Bounding the interleaving distance on concrete categories using a loss function
Astrid A. Olave, Elizabeth Munch

Abstract
The interleaving distance is arguably the most widely used metric in topological data analysis (TDA) due to its applicability to a wide array of inputs of interest, such as (multiparameter) persistence modules, Reeb graphs, merge trees, and zigzag modules. However, computation of the interleaving distance in the vast majority of this settings is known to be NP-hard, limiting its use in practical settings. Inspired by the work of Chambers et al. on the interleaving distance for mapper graphs, we solve a more general problem bounding the interleaving distance between generalized persistence modules on concrete categories via a loss function. This loss function measures how far an assignment , which can be thought of as an interleaving that might not commute, is from defining a true interleaving. We give settings for which the loss can be computed in polynomial time, including for certain assumptions on k k -parameter persistence modules.



1
Introduction

Persistent homology lies at the core of topological data analysis
(
Ghrist 2007
;
Edelsbrunner & Harer 2008
;
Oudot 2015
)
.
In its simplest form, persistence arises as follows.
Given a simplicial complex
K
together with a filtration
\{K^{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
where
K_{i}\subseteq K_{j}
for
i\leq j
, one considers the sequence of homology groups
H(K^{i})
which give rise to a
persistence module
, a family of modules over a ring indexed by
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

(
Zomorodian & Carlsson 2004
)
.
Later, these ideas were generalized to define persistence modules in categorical terms as functors from linear orders, such as
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
or
\mathbb{R}
, to the category
\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb{F}}
of finite-dimensional vector spaces over a fixed field
\mathbb{F}

(
Bubenik & Scott 2014
)
.
Generalizing this notion further, a functor from a poset
P
to a category
C
is called a
generalized persistence module
valued in
C
indexed by
P

(
Bubenik et al. 2014
)
.

The
interleaving distance

d_{I}
is one of the most used metrics to quantify the dissimilarity between generalized persistence modules.
It was first introduced by
Chazal et al. 2009
as an algebraic generalization of the combinatorial bottleneck distance for persistence diagrams.
Since it simplifies to the bottleneck distance, the interleaving distance
for persistence modules in
\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb{F}}
over
\mathbb{R}
, can be computed efficiently in polynomial time.
Since then, the interleaving distance has been generalized for different posets and categories.
When
P=\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
C=\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb{F}}
, this gives the interleaving distance for multiparameter persistence modules
(
Lesnick 2015
)
.
These functors can represent graph based objects by using
C=\mathrm{Set}
.
For this,
P=(\mathbb{R},\leq)
we have an interleaving distance for merge trees
(
Morozov et al. 2013
;
Beurskens et al. 2025
)
, and when
P=\mathrm{Int}
gives an interleaving distance for Reeb graphs
(
de Silva et al. 2016
)
.
There are also notions of labeled versions of these inputs, such as labeled merge trees
(
Munch & Stefanou 2019
;
Gasparovic et al. 2024
;
Yan et al. 2019
;
Curry et al. 2022
)
, labeled Reeb graphs
(
Lan et al. 2024
)
and formigrams
(
Kim & Mémoli 2017
)
.
The interleaving distance and its variants has also been studied for
filtered spaces
(
Blumberg & Lesnick 2017
)
,
pushforward and pullback maps
(
Botnan et al. 2020
)
,
zigzag persistence
(
Botnan & Lesnick 2018
;
Bjerkevik 2021
)
,
and dynamic metric spaces
(
Kim & Mémoli 2020
;
Kim & Mémoli 2017
)
.
Removing the functorial object assumption means that the interleaving distance has been studied for more general categorical formulations
(
de Silva et al. 2018
;
McFaddin & Needham 2025
;
Meehan & Meyer 2017
;
Cruz 2019
;
Scoccola 2020
;
Berkouk & Petit 2019
;
Patel 2018
)
.

While the interleaving distance is widely used in theory, it’s ability to be used in practice is limited due to its computational complexity in the vast majority of settings.
Other than single parameter persistence modules (where the interleaving distance is the bottleneck distance), labeled merge trees, and formigrams, nearly every other construction is NP-hard to compute
(
Bjerkevik & Botnan 2018
;
Bjerkevik et al. 2020
;
Agarwal et al. 2018
)
.
Despite the fact that one will not find an efficient algorithm unless
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{NP}
, there has been active recent progress on finding work-arounds to make computation possible
(
Curry et al. 2022
;
Pegoraro 2025
;
Farahbakhsh Touli & Wang 2019
;
Chambers et al. 2025
;
Chambers et al. 2026
)
.
In this paper, we take inspiration from the approach of
Chambers et al. 2025
.
There, the focus was on the interleaving distance for discretizations of Reeb graphs called mapper graphs.
The interleaving distance is defined on generalized persistence modules taking value in
\mathrm{Set}
, and so an interleaving is a pair of natural transformations with particular properties.
In that paper, they study collections of maps with the potential to be an interleaving but which might fail to satisfy the required commutativity conditions; these are called
assignments
(see the related concept for general sheaves in
Robinson 2020
).
Using these assignments, they established an upper bound on the interleaving distance between mapper graphs by defining a loss function which measures how far the input assignments are from commuting.
That loss function was combined with a linear programming framework in
Chambers et al. 2026
to find experimentally optimal interleavings between mapper graphs.

Our contributions

In this paper, we extend the idea of assignments from
Chambers et al. 2025
to generalized persistence modules valued in a concrete category.
Specifically following
de Silva et al. 2018
, we start with a pair of persistence modules
F,G\colon P\to C
and a flow
\mathcal{T}
where
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{T}(\varepsilon)
is an endomorphism of
P
, i.e.
\mathcal{T}\colon[0,\infty)\to\text{End}(P)
.
A pair of natural transformations

\Phi\colon F\Rightarrow G\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
and

\Psi\colon G\Rightarrow F\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}

are a
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
-interleaving of
F
and
G
if

F[p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p](p)=\Phi_{p}\Psi_{\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p}\quad\text{and}\quad G[p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p](p)=\Psi_{p}\Phi_{\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p}\text{ for all }p\in P.

We set
d_{I}(F,G)
, their interleaving distance, to be the infimum of the
\varepsilon
’s such that there is an
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
-interleaving of
F
and
G
.
A collection of morphisms
\varphi=\{\varphi_{p}\colon F(p)\rightarrow G\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}(p)\}
and
\psi=\{\psi_{p}\colon G(p)\rightarrow F\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}(p)\}
are called an
\varepsilon
-assignment if they do not necessarily define a
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
-interleaving.
We define a pseudometric for elements in each
F(p)
(sym. each
G(p)
) called the merging distance (Def.
3.1
).
Using this distance, we can define a loss function generalizing that of
(
Chambers et al. 2025
)
in Defn.
3.10
for a given input
\varepsilon
-assignment, (
\varphi,\psi
), that measures how far is the assignment from being an interleaving.
We show that this loss,
L(\varphi,\psi)
, provides an upper bound for the interleaving distance between persistence modules:
d_{I}(F,G)\leq\varepsilon+L(\varphi,\psi)
(Thm.
3.15
).

Then, for reasonable additional conditions, we optimize the computation of
L(\varphi,\psi)
for persistence modules by indexed by
P
(Thm.
4.11
) or
P^{k}
(Thm.
4.20
) controlling the number of diagrams should be checked when
P
is a complete linear order.
This makes it possible to give polynomial time algorithms for the computation for persistence modules valued in categories
C
whose objects have underlying finite sets (Alg.
1
) or in
\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb{F}}
(Alg.
2
).
Our result opens up the potential for an algorithmic approach to bound from above the interleaving distance on commonly used constructions such as multiparameter persistence modules using this context.
However, we note that, as in the case of
(
Chambers et al. 2025
;
Chambers et al. 2026
)
, this bound will only be as good as the input assignment provided so we make no promises of tightness.
Additional care will be needed in the future to find ways to search the space of assignments to optimize this bound; however, because we do not expect to accidentally prove
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{NP}
in the course of this work, any of these search algorithms will not be in
P
otherwise the combination of the bound and the search algorithm would compute the interleaving distance in polynomial time.

Outline

In Section
2
we provide a background on posets, category theory and interleaving distance for generalized persistence modules.
Section
3
we define the loss function
L
and bound the interleaving distance.
The optimization to compute
L
for different settings generalized persistence modules is done in Section
4
.
Finally, we discuss some implications from our results in Section
5
.

2
Background

2.1
Posets

We start by setting the notation and definitions for posets
(
Harzheim 2007
;
Schröder 2016
)
.
A
partially ordered set (poset)
is a pair
(P,\leq)
where
P
is a set with a reflexive, antisymmetric and transitive relation
\leq
on
P
.
We say that
p,q\in P
are
comparable
if
p\leq q
or
q\leq p
.

A subset
S\subset P
has an
upper bound

p
if
p\geq s
for all
s\in S
.
The
supremum
(or least upper bound) of
S
, if it exists, is an element
\sup S\in P
such that for any
p
which is an upper bound of
S
,
\sup S\leq p
.
Similarly, for a given set
S
we can define a
lower bound
and the
infimum
(or greatest lower bound) by reversing the inequalities in the previous definition.
A poset is called
complete
if every nonempty subset that has an upper bound has a supremum and every nonempty subset that has a lower bound has infimum.
A subset
S\subset P
is
convex
if for any
p,q\in S
and any
r\in P
with
p<r<q
, then
r\in S
.

We say
P
is a
dense poset
if for any
p<q
there is an intermediate element
p<r<q
, otherwise we say
P
is discrete.
In the discrete case, if
p<q
in
P
and there is no
r\in P
such that
p<r<q
, then
p
is a
predecessor
of
q
and
q
is a
successor
of
p
; this is denoted
p\prec q
.
Points
p
and
q
that satisfy
p\prec q
or
p\prec q
will also be called
adjacent
.
We will denote by
d(P)\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
the maximum number of predecessors of any element of
P
.

A non-empty sequence
\gamma
of
x_{i}^{\prime}s
in
P
is called a
chain
if all
x_{i}
’s in
\gamma
are comparable. If
P
itself is a chain we call it a
total
or
linear
poset.
For example, the set
[0,\infty)
with the usual order is a linear poset.
We can form another linear poset
[0,\infty]=[0,\infty)\cup\{\infty\}
by adding the element
\infty
to
[0,\infty)
and setting
\infty>r
for all
r\in[0,\infty)

The
height
of
P
,
h(P)
, is the length of the longest chain in
P
.
If there are chains of arbitrary length then
h(P)=\infty
.
A subset
S
of
P
is called an
antichain
if for all
x\neq y
in
S
,
x
and
y
are not comparable.
We define the
width
of
P
,
w(P)
, to be the size of the largest antichain in
P
if such a set exists; otherwise,
w(P)=\infty
.

Given posets
P
and
Q
, the direct product poset
P\times Q
has elements
\langle p,q\rangle
for all
p\in P
and
q\in Q
with the order relation
\langle p,q\rangle\leq_{P\times Q}\langle p^{\prime},q^{\prime}\rangle
if
p\leq_{P}p^{\prime}
and
q\leq_{Q}q^{\prime}
.
In particular, for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
we can consider the the
k
-product poset
P^{k}:=P\times\cdots\times P=\bigtimes_{i=1}^{k}P
which has elements
\mathbf{p}=\langle p^{1},\cdots,p^{k}\rangle
. We call
p^{i}
the
i
-th coordinate of
\mathbf{p}
.
Several properties of posets are inherited by their product.
For example, the product of complete posets is complete and the product of two convex subsets is convex

Following
Botnan & Crawley-Boevey 2020
, an
interval

I
of
P
is a non-empty convex connected subset of
P
.
Given
p\subset P
we define its
open initial segment
,
(-\infty,p)
, and
open final segment
,
(p,\infty)
, by

(-\infty,p)=\{q\in P\mid q<p\},\qquad(p,\infty)=\{q\in P\mid q>p\}

Analogously we define
(-\infty,p]=\{q\leq p\mid q\in P\}
and
[p,\infty)=\{q\geq p\mid q\in P\}
and call it the
closed initial (resp. final) segment
of
p
.
These four classes of sets are called the segments of
P
.
The segments of
P
as well as their intersections are intervals of
P
; for example for
p\leq q

\displaystyle[p,q]

\displaystyle:=[p,\infty)\cap(-\infty,q],\text{ the usual closed interval with ends }p,q

\displaystyle(p,q)

\displaystyle:=[p,\infty)\cap(-\infty,q),\text{ the usual open interval with ends }p,q

For
p,q\in P
, we use the notation
\lfloor p,q\rceil
to denote any interval of the form
[p,q],(p,q),(p,q]
, or
[p,q)
.

In a complete linear poset
T
, any interval is either a segment (
(\infty,q)
,
(\infty,q]
,
(p,\infty)
or
[p,\infty)
) or of the form
\lfloor p,q\rceil
for some
p<q\in T
.
However that is not the case for other posets.
The set
\{x\mid x^{2}<5\}\subset\mathbb{Q}
is an interval of
\mathbb{Q}
that can not be written as an usual interval of the form
\lfloor p,q\rceil
.
Similarly, the set
\{\langle 0,0\rangle,\langle 0,1\rangle,\langle 1,0\rangle\}\subset\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
is an interval of
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
but is not of the form
\lfloor p,q\rceil
.

Definition 2.1
.

The
order topology
of
P
is the topology generated by the subbase of open segments of
P
, given by
\{(p,\infty)\mid p\in P\}\cup\{(-\infty,q)\mid q\in Q\}
.

For example, the usual topology of
\mathbb{R}
coincides with its order topology as a poset
(\mathbb{R},\leq)
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
T
be a complete linear poset.
Any interval
[s,t]\subseteq T
is compact in the order topology of
T
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a given open cover of
[s,t]
.
Define
B
to be the set of elements
r\in[s,t]
such that
[s,r]
can be covered by a finite number of elements of
\mathcal{U}
.
The set
B
is not empty since
s\in B
and is bounded by
t
.
Then, because
T
is a complete poset,
\sup B
exists.
If
\sup B<t
we can find an interval
(x,y)\subset U\in\mathcal{U}
such that
\sup B\in(x,y)\subset[s,t]
.
Then
y\in B
, but it contradicts the definition of supremum.
Hence
t\leq\sup B
so
[s,t]
is compact.
∎

2.2
Categorical terminology

We give basic definitions for the categorical terminology used in this paper; see
Riehl 2017
for details.
A
category

C
consists of a collection of objects, e.g.
X,Y,Z\in C
, and for any pair of objects
X,Y
a collection (possibly empty) of morphisms denoted
C(X,Y)
.
We often write
f:X\to Y
if
f\in C(X,Y)
. We say that the
source
of
f
is
X
and its
target
is
Y
. There must be an identity morphism
\mathrm{Id}_{X}\in C(X,X)
for all objects
X\in C
. Further, there is a composition operation on morphisms
g\circ f\in C(X,Z)
for
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
which is associative.
A
subcategory

S
of
C
is a subset of objects and morphisms which is itself a category.
A subcategory
S\subseteq C
is
full
if
S(X,Y)=C(X,Y)
for any objects
X,Y\in S
.

For example, we write
\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb{F}}
for the category of finite-dimensional vector spaces over a fixed field
\mathbb{F}
with the morphisms given by linear transformations between vector spaces.
Another common example is that of the category of sets with set maps, denoted
\mathrm{Set}
.

We can also obtain a category from any poset
P
.
This category has an object for every element
p\in P
and a single morphism
p\to q
for any pair
p\leq q
in
P
.
This category is a
thin
category which means there is at most one morphism for any pair of objects.

A
functor

F:C\to D
between categories
C
and
D
is a map which takes objects
X
of
C
to objects
F(X)
in
D
and morphisms
f
in
C(X,Y)
to morphisms
F[f]
in
D(F(X),F(Y))
.
These mappings are compatible with the identity morphism and the associative property of the composition.

A special type of functor is a
diagram
on
C
, a functor to
C
from a category
J
with a (very) small set of objects and a (very) small set of morphisms.
The category
J
is called the index category of the diagram.
Visually, a diagram is a directed graph with objects of
C
as vertices morphisms as edges.
See Fig.
1
.
We say that the diagram commutes if any two paths of composable arrows in the directed graph with common source and target have the same composition.
For instance, the diagram of Fig.
1
commutes if
h\circ f=i\circ g
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Figure 1:
A index category
J
and square diagram on a category
C
with objects
W,X,Y,Z
and morphisms
f,g,h,i

In particular, a finite poset category is a small category. Thus for for a finite poset
P
and a category
C
, a functor
F:P\to C
is a diagram of
C
. Let us add a useful, immediate lemma that applies for the poset categories.

Lemma 2.3

(Thin lemma)
.

In a thin category all diagrams commute given there is at most one morphism between any two objects.

As a consequence of Lem.
2.3
and the definition of functors we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 2.4
.

For a finite poset
P
, a category
C
,the diagram
F:P\to C
and
p\leq r\leq q
in
P
we have that

F[p\leq q]=F[(r\leq q)\circ(p\leq r)]=F[r\leq q]\circ F[p\leq r].

There are many special cases of morphisms to consider.
A morphism
f:X\to Y
is called a
monomorphism
if for all objects
Z\in C
and morphisms
g,h:Z\to X
such that
f\circ h=f\circ g
, then
g=h
.
Similarly,
f
is called an
epimorphism
if for all objects
Z
and morphisms
h,g:Y\to Z
such that
h\circ g=g\circ f
, then
g=h
.
Finally,
f
is an
isomorphism
if there is
g:Y\to X
such that
f\circ g=id_{Y}
and
g\circ f=id_{X}
.
Note that if
f
is an isomorphism then it is monomorphism and epimorphism as well.
For example, in
\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb{F}}
the monomorphisms coincide with injective maps and epimorphism coincide with surjective maps.
A map is bijective if it is injective and surjective.
In this category, bijective maps coincide with the isomorphisms.

Similarly to morphisms, we have different types of functors.
Let
C
and
D
be locally small categories. A functor
F:C\to\mathcal{D}
is
faithful
if the mapping
C(X,Y)\to D(F(X),F(Y))
is injective and is
full
if such mapping is surjective.
We say
C
is a a
concrete category
if it is equipped with a faithful functor
U:C\to\mathrm{Set}
, also called a forgetful functor.
If
C
is a concrete category we can think of its objects as sets with additional structure and of its morphisms as structure-preserving functions.
We will call a concrete category whose underlying sets are finite a
FC category
.

Take
F,G:C\to D
to be functors. A
natural transformation

\varphi:F\Rightarrow G
consists of a family of morphisms indexed by the objects of
C
,
\{\varphi_{X}:F(X)\to G(X)\mid X\in C\}
, such that for
f:X\to Y
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(Y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(Y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[f]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G[f]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{Y}}

commutes.
If
\varphi_{X}
is an isomorphism for all objects
X
, we say that
\varphi
is a natural isomorphism.
For fixed
C
and
D
, there is a
functor category
, written
D^{C}
, with objects given by functors and morphisms given by natural transformations.
We write
\text{End}(C)=C^{C}
for the
endomorphisms
of a category
C
.

We have the following corollary of the Thin Lemma (Lem.
2.3
).

Corollary 2.5
.

Fix a poset
P
, any category
C
and functors
F,G:C\to P
.
There is a natural transformation
\varphi:F\Rightarrow G
if and only if
F(X)\leq G(X)
for all
X\in C
.
We write
F\leq G
if such a
\varphi
exists.

Take a finite poset
P
and let
P^{\text{op}}
denote its opposite category. The objects of
P^{\text{op}}
are the elements
p\in P
and for
p\leq q
in
P
there is a single morphism
q\to p
. Consider a diagram
F:P^{\text{op}}\to C
on some category
C
.

Take
N
and object of
C
and define the functor
\Delta_{N}:P^{\text{op}}\to C
given by
\Delta_{N}(p)=N
for all
p\in P
. A
cone
from
N
to
F
is a natural transformation
\varphi:\Delta_{N}\Rightarrow F
. We say that
N
is the
vertex
of the cone, as shown in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(q)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(p)}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{p}}

Let
L\in C
. We say that
L
is the
inverse limit
of
F
if there is
\psi:\Delta_{L}\Rightarrow F
a cone from
L
to
F
and for any other cone
\varphi:\Delta_{N}\Rightarrow F
there is a unique morphism
u
such that for all
p\in P
,
\psi_{p}\circ u=\varphi_{p}
. We denote it by
L=\displaystyle\lim_{\longleftarrow}(F(p)\mid p\in P)
.
The defining property of the inverse limit is called a
universal property
because any other cones factor through the inverse limit; i.e.
\psi\circ u=\varphi
.

2.3
Flows on a poset

Since our goal is to define the interleaving distance for functor categories
C^{P}
, we now discuss how to define a flow on a poset, following
Bubenik et al. 2014
and
de Silva et al. 2018
.
A
translation
on
P
,
\mathcal{S}\in\text{End}(P)
, is an endomorphism of poset
P
such that
\mathrm{Id}\leq\mathcal{S}
where
\mathrm{Id}:P\to P
is the identity endomorphism.
Let
\mathrm{Trans}(P)
denote the set of translations of
P
, where we view
\mathrm{Trans}(P)
as a full subcategory of
\text{End}(P)
.

Definition 2.6
.

A
flow

\mathcal{T}
on a poset
P
consists of a functor

\mathcal{T}:([0,\infty),\leq)\to\mathrm{Trans}(P)

with
\mathcal{T}(\varepsilon)=\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
such that

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\leq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{\prime}}
.
We say that
\mathcal{T}
is
commutative
if additionally

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
.
We call
\mathcal{T}
a
strict flow
if
\mathcal{T}_{0}=\mathrm{Id}
and

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}=\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{\prime}}
.
Explicitly, a
flow
is a function
\mathcal{T}:([0,\infty),\leq)\to\text{End}(P)
satisfying four conditions:

1.

For all
0\leq\varepsilon
and for all
p\leq q\in P
,
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}q
.

2.

For all
0\leq\delta<\varepsilon
and for all
p\in P
,
\mathcal{T}_{\delta}p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p
.

3.

For all
0\leq\varepsilon
and for all
p\in P
,
p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p
.

4.

For all
0\leq\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
and for all
p\in P
,
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{\prime}}p
.

We are going to be particularly interested in the translations attained by finitely many compositions of morphisms from some input
\mathcal{T}
, defined as follows.

Definition 2.7
.

The full subcategory
\mathrm{Flows}_{\mathcal{T}}\subseteq\mathrm{Trans}(P)\subseteq\text{End}(P)
is given by the translations of
P
that are finite compositions of morphisms
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
,

\mathrm{Flows}_{\mathcal{T}}=\{\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{\ell}}\mid\ell\geq 0,\varepsilon_{i}\geq 0\}.

If the flow is commutative (i.e.
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
), the composition in
\mathrm{Flows}_{\mathcal{T}}
is commutative (i.e., for
\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Flows}_{\mathcal{T}}
we have
\mathcal{S}\circ\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\circ\mathcal{S}
).

Definition 2.8
.

We say that a flow
\mathcal{T}
on poset
P
is an
Archimedean flow
if for all
p<q
in
P
there is
\delta
such that
\mathcal{T}_{\delta}\;p\geq q
.

For example, define the flow

\mathcal{T}:[0,\infty)\to\mathrm{Trans}(\mathbb{R})
by
T_{\varepsilon}(x)=x+\varepsilon
.
For
x<y
, we can check that
\mathcal{T}_{y-x}(x)=y
, so
\mathcal{T}
is Archimedean.
We have the following result in the special case that
\mathcal{T}
is a flow on
\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 2.9
.

A flow
\mathcal{T}
on
\mathbb{R}
is Archimedean if and only if for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
there is
M_{x}>0
such that if
\varepsilon\geq M_{x}
, then
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}(x)>x
.

Proof.

The "only if" statement is proved by contrapositive.
Take a flow with an
x
such that for all
M>0
and
\varepsilon\leq M
,
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}(x)\leq x
. Then, for all
M>0
,
x\leq\mathcal{T}_{M}(x)\leq x
. Such flow fails to be Archimedean given that for
y>x
there is no
\varepsilon
with
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}(x)\geq y
.

For the "if" statement take
\mathcal{T}
to be a flow on
\mathbb{R}
with the condition that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
there is
M_{x}>0
such that if
\varepsilon\geq M_{x}
, then
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}(x)>x
.
We can write
\mathcal{T}
as a function
\mathcal{T}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Using Condition 3), define
\varphi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
by
\varphi(\varepsilon,x)=\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}(x)-x
.
By Conditions 1) and 2),
\varphi
is non-decreasing in both entries.
Further, because of our assumptions on
\mathcal{T}
,
\varphi(\varepsilon,x)>0
for
\varepsilon\geq M_{x}

Then, by condition 4)

\displaystyle x+\varphi(\varepsilon^{\prime},x)+\varphi(\varepsilon,x)

\displaystyle\leq x+\varphi(\varepsilon^{\prime},x)+\varphi(\varepsilon,x+\varphi(\varepsilon^{\prime},x))\qquad\text{(Non decr in 2nd entry)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x)\qquad\text{(Condition (4))}

\displaystyle=x+\varphi(\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{\prime},x).

Thus,

\varphi(\cdot,x)
is superadditive, meaning
\varphi(\varepsilon^{\prime},x)+\varphi(\varepsilon,x)\leq\varphi(\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{\prime},x)
.
We prove by induction that for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\varphi(\varepsilon,x)\leq\varphi(n\varepsilon,x)
.

By way of contradiction, suppose that
\mathcal{T}
is not Archimedean so there exists
x<y
in
\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}x<y
for all
\varepsilon>0
.
Hence, for a fixed but arbitrary
\varepsilon\geq M_{x}
,

y>\mathcal{T}_{n\varepsilon}(x)=x+\varphi(n\varepsilon,x)\geq x+n\varphi(\varepsilon,x).

Dividing by
n
and taking the limit when
n
goes to infinity we obtain that
0\geq\varphi(\varepsilon,x)
.
However, this contradicts that
\varphi(\varepsilon,x)>0
, finishing the proof.
∎

Definition 2.10
.

Let
T
be linear poset.
A translation
\mathcal{S}
on
T^{k}
is called
line-preserving
if
\mathcal{S}
satisfies if it satisfies either of the equivalent conditions of the subsequent lemma.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
T
be linear poset and
\mathcal{S}
a translation on
T^{k}
. The following are equivalent:

1.

For each
1\leq i\leq k
there are translations
\mathcal{S}^{i}:T\to T
such that for
\mathbf{p}=\langle{p}^{1},\ldots,{p}^{k}\rangle\in T^{k}
we have that
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{p})=\langle\mathcal{S}^{1}(p^{1}),...,\mathcal{S}^{k}(p^{k})\rangle
.

2.

For any
i
between
1
and
k
, if
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}
in
T^{k}
are such that all their coordinates are the same except for
i
-th coordinate, then the coordinates of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{p})
and
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{q})
are the same except, maybe, for the
i
-th coordinate.

Proof.

The statement i)
\implies
ii) is straightforward.

For the reverse implication assume ii). Take a fixed
j
between
1
and
k
. Let us prove that for
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}
in
T^{k}
such that
p^{j}=q^{j}
we have that
(\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{p}))^{j}=(\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{q}))^{j}
.
Then we can define
S^{j}(t)
to be this common values for any
\mathbf{p}\in T^{k}
with
p^{j}=t
.

Take different
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}
satisfying that condition in
T^{k}
. Consider the non-empty set
U=\{i\mid p^{i}=q^{i}\}
. Let us proceed by reverse induction. If
|U|=k-1
, by assumption we obtain then
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{p})
and
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{q})
are the same except, maybe, for the coordinate not in
U
. In particular,
(\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{p}))^{j}=(\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{q}))^{j}
. Assume that if
k-1\geq|U|\geq\ell
it implies that
(\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{p}))^{j}=(\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{q}))^{j}
. Suppose that
|U|=\ell-1
. For fixed
j^{\prime},j^{\prime\prime}\notin U
choose
\mathbf{r}
with
r^{i}=p^{i}
if
i\in U
,
r^{j^{\prime}}=p^{j^{\prime}}
and
r^{j^{\prime\prime}}=q^{j^{\prime\prime}}
. Then, the sets
\{i\mid p^{i}=r^{i}\}
and
\{i\mid q^{i}=r^{i}\}
have a cardinality strictly greater than
\ell-1
, hence

(\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{p}))^{j}=(\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{r}))^{j}=(\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{q}))^{j}.

∎

Consider the example map
\mathcal{T}:[0,\infty)\to\mathrm{Trans}(\mathbb{N}^{2})
given by

T_{\varepsilon}(\langle m,n\rangle)=\langle m,n+\lfloor\varepsilon\rfloor m\rangle.

(1)

Let us check that
\mathcal{T}
is a flow checking each condition from Defn.
2.6
.

1.

Take
0\leq\varepsilon
and
\langle m,n\rangle\leq\langle m^{\prime},n^{\prime}\rangle
. Then
\langle m,n+\lfloor\varepsilon\rfloor m\rangle\leq\langle m^{\prime},n^{\prime}+\lfloor\varepsilon\rfloor m^{\prime}\rangle
.

2.

Take
0\leq\delta<\varepsilon
and any
\langle m,n\rangle
. Then
\langle m,n+\lfloor\delta\rfloor m\rangle\leq\langle m,n+\lfloor\varepsilon\rfloor m\rangle
.

3.

Take
0<\varepsilon
and any
\langle m,n\rangle
. Then
\langle m,n\rangle\leq\langle m,n+\lfloor\varepsilon\rfloor m\rangle
.

4.

Take
0<\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
and any
\langle m,n\rangle
. Then
\langle m,n+\lfloor\varepsilon\rfloor m+\lfloor\varepsilon^{\prime}\rfloor m\rangle\leq\langle m,n+\lfloor\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{\prime}\rfloor m\rangle
.

By Lemma
2.11
, the translation
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
of Eqn. (
1
) for
\varepsilon\geq 1
is not line-preserving given that
n+\lfloor\varepsilon\rfloor m
is not independent of
m
in that case.

Note that a line preserving flow is not necessarily injective.
For example, consider
\mathcal{T}:[0,\infty)\to\mathrm{Trans}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
given by
T_{0}(\langle m,n\rangle)=\langle m,n\rangle
and
T_{\varepsilon}(\langle m,n\rangle)=\langle m+\varepsilon,\lceil n\rceil+\lfloor\varepsilon\rfloor\rangle
for
\varepsilon>0
. This is line preserving because the function
m+\epsilon
is independent of
n
and the function
\lceil n\rceil+\lfloor\varepsilon\rfloor
is independent of
m
, but is not injective because
T_{\varepsilon}(\langle 1,1.5\rangle)=T_{\varepsilon}(\langle 1,2\rangle)
.

2.4
Generalized persistence modules

For a poset
P
and a category
C
, functors
F:P\to C
are called
generalized persistence modules
in
C
over
P

(
Bubenik et al. 2014
)
.
The collection of these functors is itself a (thin) category with natural transformations as morphisms, denoted
C^{P}
.

The terminology arises from the following standard construction in persistence.
Let
\emptyset=K_{0}\subseteq K_{1}\subseteq\cdots K_{n}=K
be a filtration of a simplicial complex
K
.
Denote by
H_{k}(-)
the
k
th homology with coefficients in some field
\mathbb{F}
. Define the functor:
F:\{0,\ldots,n\}\to\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb{F}}
given by
F(i):=H_{k}(K_{i})
and
F[i\leq j]:=H_{k}(K_{i})\to H_{k}(K_{j})
the map induced by
K_{i}\hookrightarrow K_{j}
.
Then,
F
is a generalized persistence module in
\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb{F}}
over
\{0,\ldots,n\}
.
A module
F:P\to\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb{F}}
is called a
q
-tame module
if for all
r<p\in P
the corresponding maps
F[r\leq p]
have finite rank.
1
1

1

We note that the term
q
-tame is common in the literature (e.g.
Oudot 2015
, Def 1.12
), it is not related to any fixed choice of
q\in P
.

For another example, consider the same filtration from before.
Define the functor
G:\{0,\ldots,n\}\to\mathrm{Set}
given by
G(i):=\pi_{0}(K_{i})
the set of connected components of
K_{i}
with morphisms
G(i)\to G(j)
induced by inclusion.
Such module is called a
merge tree
since it is the categorical representation of the eponymous topological construction that tracks the connectivity of
K
over the filtration.

2.4.1
Critical values of a persistence module

Definition 2.12
.

Let
F\in C^{P}
be a generalized persistence module and let
I\subseteq P
be an interval.
We say that
F
is
constant on

I
, or that
I
is a constant interval of
F
if for all
p\leq q\in I
,
F[p\leq q]
is an isomorphism.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
T
be a linear poset and fix
t\in T
. A subset
I\subseteq T
is called an
open interval around

t
if:

•

The set
I
is equal to
(x,y)
,
(x,\infty)
or
(-\infty,y)
for some
x<t<y\in T
.

•

Whenever
t
has an upper bound in
T
, there exists
u\in I
with
t<u
.

•

Whenever
t
has a lower bound in
T
, there exists
s\in I
with
s<t
.

Thus,
I
is an open interval in the usual sense and includes
t
, one element above
t
(when one exists) and one element below
t
(when one exists).
For example, in the case where
T=\mathbb{Z}
, the set
I=\{2\}\subset\mathbb{Z}
is of the form
(1,3)
, however, it is not an open interval around
t=2
because there is no
u\in I
for which
2<u
.
The set
(0,4)
however would be a open interval around
t=2
.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
T
be a complete linear poset and fix a
F\in C^{T}
.
We say that
t\in T
is a
critical value
of
F
if for any open interval around
t
,
F
is not constant on
I
.
Otherwise, we say that
t
is a
regular value
of
F
.
We call
F\in C^{T}

tame
if it has a finite number of critical values.
In that case, the set of all critical values of
F
,
\{c_{1},\dots,c_{m}\}
, is called the
critical set
of
F
.

The notions of critical values and tame modules in categorical terms were introduced by Bubenik and Scott in
Bubenik & Scott 2014
for
P=\mathbb{R}
.
The definitions presented here apply to any complete linear poset.
We can consider
\mathbb{R}
to be complete adding
-\infty
and
\infty
as the minimal, respectively maximal of the order.

Lemma 2.15

(Critical Value Lemma)
.

An interval
I\subseteq T
does not contain any critical values of
F:T\to C
, if and only if
F
is constant on
I
.

Proof.

Let
I
be an interval that does not contain a critical point. The sufficient condition follows from the Critical Value Lemma (4.4) of
Bubenik & Scott 2014
, Lem. 4.4
join with the Lemma
2.2
to satisfy the proper conditions.
The necessary condition comes by the fact that an interval containing a critical point of
F
can not be a constant interval of
F
.
∎

Definition 2.16
.

Fix a complete linear poset
T
and a generalized persistence module
F:T^{k}\to C
.
We say that
t\in T
is a
critical
j
-coordinate
of
F
if there are some
t_{1},\ldots,t_{j-1},t_{j+1},\ldots,t_{k}\in T
such that
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{j-1},t,t_{j+1},\ldots,t_{k})
is a critical value of
F_{j}:T\to C;s\mapsto F((t_{1},\ldots,t_{j-1},s,t_{j+1},\ldots t_{k}))

Definition 2.17
.

We call
F:T^{k}\to C

cubical tame
if it has a finite number of critical coordinates.

2.4.2
Distance between persistence modules

We next define a distance in
C^{P}
using the notion of flows from Sec.
2.3
.

Definition 2.18
.

Assume the poset
P
is endowed with a commutative flow
\mathcal{T}
.
We say that
F,G\in C^{P}
are
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
-interleaved if we have a pair of natural transformations

\Phi:F\Rightarrow G\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\qquad\Psi:G\Rightarrow F\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}

such that the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G[p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p]}

commute.
The interleaving distance is defined to be

d_{I}(F,G)=\inf\{\varepsilon\geq 0\mid F,G\text{ are }\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\text{-interleaved}\}

and we set
d_{I}(F,G)=\infty
if there is no
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
-interleaving.

Proposition 2.19

(
Bubenik et al. 2014
, Thm. 3.15
)
.

The interleaving distance
d_{I}
is an extended-pseudometric on
C^{P}
.

3
General loss function bounding the interleaving distance

In this section, we give a bound for the interleaving distance for generalized persistence modules by defining a loss function inspired by
Chambers et al. 2025
.
Throughout this section, we assume that
F
and
G
are generalized persistence modules in
C^{P}
for a concrete category
C
and a poset
P
with a commutative flow
\mathcal{T}
.

3.1
The merging distance function

Fix
p\in P
.
We will build a notion of distance on
F(p)
by measuring how much "we need to flow" on
P
to attain an element equality.
For that reason, we will assume that
\mathcal{T}_{0}=\mathrm{Id}
.

Definition 3.1
.

For
a,b\in F(p)
, define

d_{p}^{F}(a,b)=\inf\{\varepsilon\geq 0\mid F[p\leq T_{\varepsilon}p](a)=F[p\leq T_{\varepsilon}p](b)\}.

Since the function is indeed a distance (which will be shown in Theorem
3.4
), we call it the
merging distance
function.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
a,b\in F(p)
.
If there is
\mathcal{S}\in\mathrm{Trans}(P)
such that

F[p\leq\mathcal{S}\,p](a)=F[p\leq\mathcal{S}\,p](b)
,
then for any
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
,

F[p\leq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\,p](a)=F[p\leq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\,p](b)
.

This can be restated as follows: if the translation by
\mathcal{S}
in
F
attains the equality of
a
and
b
, then the equality is attained by any translation
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\geq\mathcal{S}
.

Proof of Lem.
3.2
.

We compute

F[p\leq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}p](a)=F[\mathcal{S}\,p\leq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}p]F[p\leq\mathcal{S}p](a)=F[\mathcal{S}\,p\leq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\,p]F[p\leq\mathcal{S}\,p](b)=F[p\leq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\,p](b)

where the first and third equality follow from Cor.
2.4
and the second equality follows from the assumption.
∎

Then we have the following immediate corollary.

Corollary 3.3
.

For any
\delta>d_{p}^{F}(a,b)
we have that
F[p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\delta}p](a)=F[p\leq T_{\delta}p](b)
.

Next, we check the distance properties of
d_{p}^{F}
.

Theorem 3.4
.

The merging distance
d_{p}^{F}
is an extended pseudo-ultrametric on
F(p)
.

Proof.

For any
a,b\in P
, it is immediate from the definition that
d_{p}^{F}(a,b)=d_{p}^{F}(b,a)
, that
d_{p}^{F}(a,b)\geq 0
, and that
d_{p}^{F}(a,a)=0
.
However it can be the case that
d_{p}^{F}(a,b)=0
but
a
and
b
are not equal, see Example
3.5
.
The distance can take infinite values since
d_{p}^{F}(a,b)=\infty
if there is no
\varepsilon\geq 0
such that
F[p\leq T_{\varepsilon}p](a)=F[p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p](b)
.

Finally we show that

d_{p}^{F}(a,b)\leq\max\{d_{p}^{F}(a,c),d_{p}^{F}(c,b)\}

for any
c\in F(p)
for the ultrametric property.
If either
d_{p}^{F}(a,c)
or
d_{p}^{F}(c,b)
are infinite we are done, so without loss of generality, assume
d_{p}^{F}(a,c)\leq d_{p}^{F}(c,b)<\infty
.
Choose
0\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}
such that

F[p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p](a)=F[p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p](c)\qquad and\qquad F[p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}p](c)=F[p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}p](b).

Then, by Lemma
3.2
,
F[p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}p](a)=F[p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}p](c)=F[p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}p](b)
. Hence, since
\varepsilon^{\prime}
is arbitrary we obtain that
d_{p}^{F}(a,b)\leq d_{p}^{F}(c,b)
.
∎

Example 3.5
.

Define the flow
\mathcal{T}:[0,\infty)\to\mathrm{Trans}([0,\infty))
by
T_{\varepsilon}(m)=m+\varepsilon
. Consider the modules
F
and
F^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{Set}^{[0,\infty)}
given by

F(i)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\{a,b\}&i<3\\
\{a\}&i\geq 3\end{array}\right.\hskip 85.35826ptF^{\prime}(i)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\{a,b\}&i\leq 3\\
\{a\}&i>3\end{array}\right.

with morphisms sending
a\to a
and
b\to b
when possible, and
b\to a
when its not.
Visually, we can think of
F
as the diagram of maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(3)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cdots}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{2}}}

and
F^{\prime}
is the same except for the map into
F(3)
.
Note that
F
and
F^{\prime}
are almost the same, they have the same critical point but change the size of sets differently on either side of 3.
We can check that
d_{i}^{F}(a,b)=d_{i}^{F^{\prime}}(a,b)=3-i
for
i<3
and
d_{3}^{F^{\prime}}(a,b)=0
. This shows we may have different elements with distance 0.

Also, notice that this distance corresponds to "how far" in terms of the flow is
i
is from
3
. This gives us that for
i<j<3
and
\delta\geq(j-i)

d_{j}^{F}(a,b)<d_{i}^{F}(a,b)\leq d_{j}^{F}(a,b)+\delta

This result is formalized in the following lemmas.

Lemma 3.6
.

If
p\leq q
in
P
and
b,b^{\prime}\in F(p)
with
c=F[p\leq q](b)
and
c^{\prime}=F[p\leq q](b^{\prime})
,
then
d_{q}^{F}(c,c^{\prime})\leq d_{p}^{F}(b,b^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Fix
\delta>d_{p}^{F}(b^{\prime},b)
.
By Corollary
3.3
we have that
F[p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\delta}p](b)=F[p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\delta}p](b^{\prime})=:x
.
By the Thin Lemma, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}b\\
b^{\prime}\end{subarray}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}c\\
c^{\prime}\end{subarray}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(T_{\delta}p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(T_{\delta}q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}

commutes taking
y=F[T_{\delta}p\leq T_{\delta}q](x).

So since
b,b^{\prime}
map to the same element,
c
and
c^{\prime}
also map to the same element, explicitly,
y=F[q\leq\mathcal{T}_{\delta}q](c)=F[q\leq\mathcal{T}_{\delta}q](c^{\prime})
.
This implies that
\delta\geq d_{q}^{F}(c^{\prime},c)
and, since the
\delta
is arbitrary, by definition of infimum the conclusion follows.
∎

Intuitively, Lemma
3.6
says that the merge distance is non-increasing when flowing forward.
Further, by setting
q=\mathcal{T}_{\delta}\;p
in Lemma
3.6
for any
\delta\in(d_{p}^{F}(b^{\prime},b),\infty)
, by Cor.
3.3
for any
c,c^{\prime}\in F(T_{\delta}\;p)=F(q)
, we find that
d_{q}^{F}(c^{\prime},c)=0
.

When it comes to bound
d_{p}^{F}(b^{\prime},b)
in terms of
d_{q}^{F}(c,c^{\prime})
the Archimedean property of the flow is a sufficient condition

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be an Archimedean flow on
P
. If
p\leq q
in
P
and
b,b^{\prime}\in F(p)
with
c=F[p\leq q](b)
and
c^{\prime}=F[p\leq q](b^{\prime})
, then, there is
\delta
such that
d_{q}^{F}(b,b^{\prime})<d_{p}^{F}(c,c^{\prime})+\delta
.

Proof.

Following the same notation as the previous lemma, we will show there is
\delta
such that
d_{p}^{F}(b,b^{\prime})<d_{q}^{F}(c,c^{\prime})+\delta
. To find such a
\delta
, take

\gamma>d_{q}^{F}(c,c^{\prime})
.
Then, by Cor.
3.3
this gives that

F[q\leq T_{\gamma}q](c)=F[q\leq T_{\gamma}q](c^{\prime})
.
Thus

\displaystyle F[p\leq T_{\gamma}q](b)

\displaystyle=F[q\leq T_{\gamma}q]F[p\leq q](b)

\displaystyle=F[q\leq T_{\gamma}q](c)

\displaystyle=F[q\leq T_{\gamma}q](c^{\prime})

\displaystyle=F[q\leq T_{\gamma}q]F[p\leq q](b^{\prime})

\displaystyle=F[p\leq T_{\gamma}q](b^{\prime}).

Because
\mathcal{T}
is a flow,

p<\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}q
, so by the Archimedean assumption, there is a
\hat{\delta}
such that
\mathcal{T}_{\hat{\delta}}p>T_{\gamma}q
.
Note that along with
\mathcal{T}_{\hat{\delta}}q\geq\mathcal{T}_{\hat{\delta}}p>T_{\gamma}q
, this implies that
\hat{\delta}>\gamma
, so

F[p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\hat{\delta}}p](b)=F[p\leq\mathcal{T}_{\hat{\delta}}p](b^{\prime})

Hence setting
\delta=\hat{\delta}-d_{q}^{F}(c,c^{\prime})
, we have

\gamma+\delta=\gamma+(\hat{\delta}-d_{q}^{F}(c,c^{\prime}))>\gamma+(\hat{\delta}-\gamma)=\hat{\delta}>d_{p}^{F}(b,b^{\prime})

Since
\gamma
was arbitrary we obtain that
d_{q}^{F}(c,c^{\prime})+\delta>d_{p}^{F}(b,b^{\prime})
finishing the proof.
∎

3.2
The loss function

Our next job is to define structures that take the form of natural transformations, but lack the usual guarantees.

Definition 3.8
.

Given functors
F,F^{\prime}\in C^{P}
, an
unnatural transformation

\eta:F\mathbin{\hskip 2.15277pt\scalebox{-1}[-1]{\hbox{\hskip 1.72221pt\hskip-1.72221pt\hbox{\raisebox{-1.6pt}{\uwave{\raisebox{2.0pt}{\hskip 3.44444pt}}}}\hskip-1.72221pt\hskip-1.72221pt\raisebox{-3.7pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.6pt}{\uwave{\raisebox{2.0pt}{\hskip 3.44444pt}}}}}\hskip-1.72221pt\hskip 1.72221pt}}\mkern-2.6mu\scalebox{.7}[1.1]{$\succ$}}F^{\prime}

is a collection of maps
\eta_{p}:F(p)\to F^{\prime}(p)
with no additional promise of commutativity.
For a fixed
\mathcal{S}\in\mathrm{Flows}_{\mathcal{T}}
(Def.
2.7
) and functors
F,G\in C^{P}
, an
\mathcal{S}
-
assignment of

F
and
G
is a pair of unnatural transformations
\varphi:F\mathbin{\hskip 2.15277pt\scalebox{-1}[-1]{\hbox{\hskip 1.72221pt\hskip-1.72221pt\hbox{\raisebox{-1.6pt}{\uwave{\raisebox{2.0pt}{\hskip 3.44444pt}}}}\hskip-1.72221pt\hskip-1.72221pt\raisebox{-3.7pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.6pt}{\uwave{\raisebox{2.0pt}{\hskip 3.44444pt}}}}}\hskip-1.72221pt\hskip 1.72221pt}}\mkern-2.6mu\scalebox{.7}[1.1]{$\succ$}}G\mathcal{S}
and
\psi:G\mathbin{\hskip 2.15277pt\scalebox{-1}[-1]{\hbox{\hskip 1.72221pt\hskip-1.72221pt\hbox{\raisebox{-1.6pt}{\uwave{\raisebox{2.0pt}{\hskip 3.44444pt}}}}\hskip-1.72221pt\hskip-1.72221pt\raisebox{-3.7pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.6pt}{\uwave{\raisebox{2.0pt}{\hskip 3.44444pt}}}}}\hskip-1.72221pt\hskip 1.72221pt}}\mkern-2.6mu\scalebox{.7}[1.1]{$\succ$}}F\mathcal{S}
which
we write as
(\varphi,\psi)
.

For fixed
p,q\in P
and
(\varphi,\psi)
a
\mathcal{S}
-assignment of
F
and
G
consider the following diagrams indexed by finite categories in the category
C
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(q)}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{p}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\mathcal{S}p}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(\mathcal{S}p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigtriangleup_{\varphi,\psi}(p):}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{S}p)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathcal{S}p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\;\leq\;]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathcal{S}p}\circ\psi_{p}}

We give names for each diagram at the left side of each diagram.
Our goal to measure the quality of a given assignment
(\varphi,\psi)
by checking how far the parallel morphisms defined in each diagram are from being equal.
This is because if they were equal, the structure in question would indeed be a natural transformation (by the first two) and an interleaving (by the second two).
This next definition writes the distance function between natural transformations using the distance already defined in the codomain, largely to simplify notation later.

Definition 3.9
.

Fix
q\in P
and let
f,g:A\to F(q)
be a pair of morphisms in
C
with source
A\in C
.
We define the distance between
f
and
g
by

d(f,g)=\sup_{a\in A}\left\{d_{q}^{F}(f(a),g(a))\right\}.

With this definition, we now have four measurements to be made for the diagrams above.

Definition 3.10
.

Fix a
\mathcal{S}
-assignment
(\varphi,\psi)
.
We define the loss of each diagram by

\displaystyle=d(\varphi_{q}\circ F[p\leq q],G[\mathcal{S}p\leq\mathcal{S}q]\circ\varphi_{p})

\displaystyle=d(\psi_{q}\circ G[p\leq q],F[\mathcal{S}p\leq\mathcal{S}q]\circ\psi_{p})

\displaystyle L_{\bigtriangleup}^{p}(\varphi,\psi)

\displaystyle=d(G[p\leq\mathcal{S}\mathcal{S}p],\varphi_{\mathcal{S}p}\circ\psi_{p}).

(2)

Note that a
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
-assignment
(\varphi,\psi)
with loss 0 is a
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
-interleaving between
F
and

.

.

, define the flow
\mathcal{T}:[0,\infty)\to\mathrm{Trans}(\mathbb{N})
by
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}(m)=m+\lfloor\varepsilon\rfloor
.
Consider the merge trees

and

in

given by

.

3.3
Bounding the interleaving distance

In this subsection, we show how a
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
-assignment with loss
\delta
induces a
\mathcal{T}_{\delta}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
-interleaving thereby bounding the interleaving distance by
\delta+\varepsilon
.

Definition 3.12
.

Let
\mathcal{S}\in\mathrm{Flows}_{\mathcal{T}}
and take
(\varphi,\psi)
a
\mathcal{S}
-assignment.
Given a translation
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{Flows}_{\mathcal{T}}
, the
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}
-assignment
(\varphi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
given by

\varphi^{\prime}=G[\mathcal{R}]\circ\varphi

and

\psi^{\prime}=F[\mathcal{R}]\circ\psi

is called the
\mathcal{R}
-translation of
(\varphi,\psi)
.

We next analyze how the
\mathcal{R}
-translation transforms the loss of the assignment
(\varphi,\psi)
.

Theorem 3.13
.

Fix
p,q
in
P
and
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}\in\mathrm{Flows}_{\mathcal{T}}
.
Let
(\varphi,\psi)
be a
\mathcal{S}
-assignment and
(\varphi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
the
\mathcal{R}
-translation of
(\varphi,\psi)
.
Then, the following holds.

1.

.

Proof.

Note that (1) and (3); and (2) and (4) are symmetric.
So we will only prove statements (1) and (2).

We start with statement (1).
Fix
a\in F(p)
.
Consider the elements

b=\varphi_{q}\circ F[p\leq q](a)
,

b^{\prime}=G[\mathcal{S}p\leq\mathcal{S}q]\circ\varphi_{p}(a^{\prime})
,

c=\varphi^{\prime}_{q}\circ F[p\leq q](a)
and

c^{\prime}=G[\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}p\leq\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}q]\circ\varphi^{\prime}_{p}(a^{\prime})
.
Such elements can be seen in the following non-commutative diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(q)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(\mathcal{S}p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(\mathcal{S}q)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}q)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[\hskip 1.50694pt\leq\hskip 1.50694pt]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi_{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi_{q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G[\;\leq\;]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G[\mathcal{R}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G[\mathcal{R}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G[\;\leq\;]}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}b\\
b^{\prime}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}c\\
c^{\prime}\end{array}}

Since by definition
\varphi^{\prime}=G[\mathcal{R}]\circ\varphi
,
the left and right triangles commute so

c=G[\mathcal{R}]\left(b\right)

and

c^{\prime}=G[\mathcal{R}]\left(b^{\prime}\right)
.
By Lemma
3.6
we have that

d_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}q}^{G}(c,c^{\prime})\leq d_{\mathcal{S}q}^{G}(b,b^{\prime}).

Therefore, by definition of the parallelogram loss and the properties of supremum we have

,
finishing statement (1).

For statement (2), let
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
and consider the following non necessarily commutative diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{S}p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\mathcal{S}p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(\mathcal{S}p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}p)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\;\leq\;]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\varphi_{p}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi^{\prime}_{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\mathcal{R}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\mathcal{R}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{||}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\psi_{\mathcal{S}p}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G[\mathcal{R}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\mathcal{R}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\psi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}p}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\psi_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}p}}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}b\\
b^{\prime}\end{array}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{l}\\
c\\
c^{\prime}\\
c^{\prime\prime}\end{array}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\\
e\\
e^{\prime}\\
e^{\prime\prime}\end{array}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}

For
a\in F(p)
set

e=F[p\leq\mathcal{S}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}p](a)
and

e^{\prime\prime}=\psi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}p}\circ\varphi^{\prime}_{p}(a)
.
Since by Thm.
3.4
the merging distance is an ultrametric, we have that

d_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}p}^{F}(e,e^{\prime\prime})\leq\max\left\{d_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}p}^{F}(e,e^{\prime}),d_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}p}^{F}(e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime})\right\}.

By Lemma
3.6
,
d_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}p}^{F}(e,e^{\prime})\leq d_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\mathcal{S}p}^{F}(c,c^{\prime})\leq d_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{S}p}^{F}(b,b^{\prime})
.
On the other hand, by the same Lemma,
d_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}p}^{F}(e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime})\leq d_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}p}^{F}(c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime})
.
Hence

d_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}p}^{F}(e,e^{\prime\prime})\leq\max\left\{d_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{S}p}^{F}(b,b^{\prime}),d_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}p}^{F}(c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime})\right\}.

By definition of the loss of the parallelogram and triangle diagrams and the definition of supremum, we conclude that

L_{\bigtriangledown}^{p}(\varphi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\leq\max\{L_{\bigtriangledown}^{p}(\varphi,\psi),L_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.7}{$\displaystyle\hbox to7.33pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.59 6.3 L 3.78 6.3 L 0 0 L 5.81 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\textstyle\hbox to7.33pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

finishing statement (2) and the proof.
∎

Intuitively, Theorem
3.13
says that the loss is non-increasing when translating by
\mathcal{R}
. Further, we can reach a zero-loss for a special
\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
- translation.

Lemma 3.14
.

Given a a
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
-assignment
(\varphi,\psi)
, choose a
\delta
such that

\delta>\max\{L_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.7}{$\displaystyle\hbox to7.33pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.93002pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.03 6.3 L -3.78 6.3 L 0 0 L 5.81 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\textstyle\hbox to7.33pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.93002pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}

Proof.

3.13
.
Since

then by Cor.

,

.
Hence

.
The symmetric argument gives that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.42 4.41 L -2.64 4.41 L 0 0 L 4.07 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\hbox to3.87pt{\vbox to2.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.565pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.02 3.15 L -1.89 3.15 L 0 0 L 2.91 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}^{p,q}(\Psi)=0
.

For the triangle loss consider the proof of Statement (2) in Thm.
3.13
.
Using Cor.
3.3
, we have that for any
a\in F(p)
,

d_{\mathcal{T}_{\delta}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{T}_{\delta}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p}^{F}(e,e^{\prime})=0
given that

\delta>L_{\bigtriangledown}^{p}(\varphi,\psi)\geq d_{\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p}^{F}(b,b^{\prime}).

Similarly
d_{\mathcal{T}_{\delta}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{T}_{\delta}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p}^{F}(e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime})=0
provided that

\delta>L_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.7}{$\displaystyle\hbox to7.33pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.59 6.3 L 3.78 6.3 L 0 0 L 5.81 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\textstyle\hbox to7.33pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.59 6.3 L 3.78 6.3 L 0 0 L 5.81 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptstyle\hbox to5.25pt{\vbox to3.59pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.79 3.15 L 1.89 3.15 L 0 0 L 2.91 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}^{\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p,\mathcal{T}_{\delta}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p}(\psi)\geq d_{T_{\delta}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p}^{F}(c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime}).

Therefore,
d_{\mathcal{T}_{\delta}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{T}_{\delta}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}p}^{F}(e,e^{\prime\prime})=0
, so,
L_{\bigtriangledown}^{p}(\Phi,\Psi)=0
.
Again symmetrically,
L_{\bigtriangledown}^{p}(\Phi,\Psi)=0
.
∎

Theorem 3.15
.

For any
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
-assignment given by
\varphi:F\mathbin{\hskip 2.15277pt\scalebox{-1}[-1]{\hbox{\hskip 1.72221pt\hskip-1.72221pt\hbox{\raisebox{-1.6pt}{\uwave{\raisebox{2.0pt}{\hskip 3.44444pt}}}}\hskip-1.72221pt\hskip-1.72221pt\raisebox{-3.7pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.6pt}{\uwave{\raisebox{2.0pt}{\hskip 3.44444pt}}}}}\hskip-1.72221pt\hskip 1.72221pt}}\mkern-2.6mu\scalebox{.7}[1.1]{$\succ$}}G\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
and
\psi:G\mathbin{\hskip 2.15277pt\scalebox{-1}[-1]{\hbox{\hskip 1.72221pt\hskip-1.72221pt\hbox{\raisebox{-1.6pt}{\uwave{\raisebox{2.0pt}{\hskip 3.44444pt}}}}\hskip-1.72221pt\hskip-1.72221pt\raisebox{-3.7pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.6pt}{\uwave{\raisebox{2.0pt}{\hskip 3.44444pt}}}}}\hskip-1.72221pt\hskip 1.72221pt}}\mkern-2.6mu\scalebox{.7}[1.1]{$\succ$}}F\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
,

d_{I}(F,G)\leq\varepsilon+L(\varphi,\psi).

Proof.

Set
\delta>L(\psi,\varphi)
. By Lemma
3.14
the
\mathcal{T}_{\delta}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
-assignment
(\Phi,\Psi)
given by
\Phi=G[\mathcal{T}_{\delta}]\circ\varphi
and
\Psi=F[\mathcal{T}_{\delta}]\circ\psi
is a
\mathcal{T}_{\delta}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
-interleaving of
F
and
G
. By monotonicity of the interleaving we have that
F
and
G
are
\mathcal{T}_{\delta+\varepsilon}
-interleaved. Therefore, as
\delta
is arbitrary, we have that
d_{I}(F,G)\leq\varepsilon+L(\psi,\varphi)
as we wanted.
∎

Example 3.16
.

Take
F,G
and
\mathcal{T}
from Example and
3.11
.
For
\varepsilon=1
, we calculated
L(\varphi,\psi)=1
, so by Theorem
3.15
we obtain that
d_{I}(F,G)\leq 2
.

We note that this theorem makes no promise of tightness, and indeed we would not expect that.
Instead, it can be viewed as providing a bound for any input assignment, and then the goal would be to find an assignment providing the best possible bound under some circumstances.
However, we leave that particular direction to future work and instead focus on determining what is necessary to compute
L(\varphi,\psi)
for a given input assignment.

4
Reducing calculations

This section is devoted to creating algorithms and strategies to calculate
L(\varphi,\psi)
using different properties of the distance function and by narrowing
P
and
\mathcal{C}
to particular cases largely of interest to the TDA community.
Throughout the section, we assume that
F,G\in\mathcal{C}^{P}
,
\mathcal{T}
is a commutative flow and and
(\varphi,\psi)
is a
\mathcal{S}
-assignment between
F
and
G
, unless said explicitly otherwise.

4.1
Improving the calculation of the merging distance function

Definition 4.1
.

For a fixed
q\in P
, we say that
\delta\in[0,\infty)
is a
reducing constant
of
q
on
F
if for all

0\leq\gamma_{1}<\delta\leq\gamma_{2}
, with
\gamma_{1}\neq\gamma_{2}
,

F[\mathcal{T}_{\gamma_{1}}q\leq\mathcal{T}_{\gamma_{2}}q]
is non-monomorphism.
Otherwise, we say that
\delta
is a
non-reducing constant
of
q
on
F
.

For
q\in P
, we write
D^{F}_{q}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
for the set of reducing constants of
q
on
F
.

Example 4.2
.

Continuing with the flow
\mathcal{T}
and the module
F
of Example
3.11
, consider the point
q=1\in\mathbb{N}
.
We will show that the set of reducing constants of
1
on
F
is
D_{1}^{F}=\{2\}
.
Note that for
0<\gamma_{1}<2
we have
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma_{1}}(1)<2
.
Meanwhile, for
\gamma_{2}\geq 2
we obtain
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma_{2}}(1)\geq 3
.
Then for such values,

F[\mathcal{T}_{\gamma_{1}}(1)\leq\mathcal{T}_{\gamma_{2}}(1)]=F[1+\lfloor\gamma_{1}\rfloor\leq 2]\circ F[2\leq 3]\circ F[3\leq 1+\lfloor\gamma_{2}\rfloor]

Hence, this morphism is not a monomorphism given that
F[2\leq 3]
is not injective. Moreover, note that this in the only non injective morphism of the form
F[i\leq i+1]
, and so there are no other elements in
D_{1}^{F}
for this example.

Our next lemma shows that the the distance can only take values in this set of reducing constants.

Lemma 4.3
.

Fix
q\in P
.
If
D^{F}_{q}
is finite, then for any
a\neq b
in
F(q)
,
d_{q}^{F}(a,b)\in D^{F}_{q}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Proof.

Let
D:=D_{q}^{F}=\{\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{m}\}
be sorted so that
\delta_{i}<\delta_{i+1}
.
Seeking a contradiction, say there is a pair
a\neq b
in
F(q)
and an
i
with

\delta_{i-1}<d_{q}^{F}(a,b)<\delta_{i}
(setting
\delta_{0}=0
if need be).
Then, for any
\delta
with
d_{q}^{F}(a,b)\leq\delta<\delta_{i}
, we have that

F[q\leq\mathcal{T}_{\delta}q](a)=F[q\leq\mathcal{T}_{\delta}q](b)
.
Since
\delta\notin D
, choose
0<\gamma<\delta
such that
F[\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}q\leq\mathcal{T}_{\delta}q]
is a monomorphism.
Recall that
F[q\leq\mathcal{T}_{\delta}q]=F[\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}q\leq\mathcal{T}_{\delta}q]\circ F[q\leq\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}q]
.
This implies that
F[q\leq\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}q](a)=F[q\leq\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}q](b)
, then
d_{q}^{F}(a,b)\leq\gamma
. Since
\delta
was arbitrary we obtain that

F[q\leq\mathcal{T}_{d_{q}^{F}(a,b)}q](a)=F[q\leq\mathcal{T}_{d_{q}^{F}(a,b)}q](b).

Then, using a similar analysis for
\delta=d_{q}^{F}(a,b)<\delta_{i}
we can find
\gamma<d_{q}^{F}(a,b)
with
F[q\leq\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}q](a)=F[q\leq\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}q](b)
, which is a contradiction. Hence, having a
\delta
between
d_{q}^{F}(a,b)
and
\delta_{i}
yields a contradiction. Therefore,
\delta_{i}=d_{q}^{F}(a,b)
.

Now, consider the case where
\delta_{n}\leq d_{q}^{F}(a,b)
.
If
d_{q}^{F}(a,b)=\infty
we are done.
If not, note that for

\delta_{n}<\delta<d_{q}^{F}(a,b)

we can find
\delta<\gamma\leq d_{q}^{F}(a,b)
where
F[\mathcal{T}_{\delta}q\leq\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}q]
is a monomorphism.
By a similar process, we can obtain a
\gamma>d_{q}^{F}(a,b)
with

F[q\leq\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}q](a)\neq F[q\leq\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}q](b)
, which is a contradiction.
Hence
\delta_{n}=d_{q}^{F}(a,b)
, finishing the proof.
∎

With this lemma, we have the following immediate theorem, which can limit the possible values of the loss function.

Theorem 4.4
.

The following hold:

1.

If
q\in P
is such that
D^{G}_{\mathcal{S}q}
is finite, then for any
p\leq q
,

is a finite set; or when the underlying sets of objects of

are finite) the set of reducing constants will always be finite.

,
L_{\bigtriangledown}^{p}(\varphi,\psi)
and
L_{\bigtriangleup}^{p}(\varphi,\psi)
are similar.

In what follows, we fix
p<q\in P
and write
D^{G}_{\mathcal{S}q}=\{\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{m}\}
.
We fix a set of interleaved values
\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i=0}^{m}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with

0=\gamma_{0}<\delta_{0}<\gamma_{1}<\cdots<\delta_{i}<\gamma_{i}<\delta_{i+1}<\cdots\delta_{m}<\gamma_{m}
.
To simplify notation in the algorithm, we write the composition morphisms on the two sides of the parallelogram by

f=\varphi_{q}\circ F[p\leq q]

and

g=G[\mathcal{S}p\leq\mathcal{S}q]\circ\varphi_{p}
, so we have

L_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.7}{$\displaystyle\hbox to7.33pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.93002pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

The idea is based on a binary search (See e.g.
Erickson 2019
), where at each step checking
\gamma_{i}
, we only keep the elements of
F(p)
which do not map to the same element at the end of diagram
\hbox to10.3pt{\vbox to6.9pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.10004pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

The basic idea is to do a binary search on the set
D_{\mathcal{S}q}^{G}
to find the lowest
\delta_{i}
for which all
a\in F(p)
have the same images
f(a)
and
g(a)
in
G(\mathcal{T}_{\delta_{i}}\mathcal{S}q)
.
This means that the time complexity of calculating

4.1.2
Calculating the loss for vector fields

We now turn to the case where there is additional structure on
\mathcal{C}
to be used, in particular, the case where
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb{F}}
for some field
\mathbb{F}
.
For this portion, consider

F,G\in\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb{F}}^{P}
to be
q
-tame modules.
As before, we we fix
p<q\in P
and write
D^{G}_{\mathcal{S}q}=\{\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{m}\}
with interleaved values
\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i=0}^{m}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

Recall that for any linear transformations,
h,h^{\prime}:V\to W
, we have that
h(v)=h^{\prime}(v)
if and only if
v\in\ker(h-h^{\prime})
. Further,
\ker(h-h^{\prime})=V
if and only if
\text{Im}(h-h^{\prime})=\{0\}
.

The linear maps
f
and
g
are identical to those from the previous section. The following algorithm find the loss of the diagram based again on binary search. This time, it checks
\gamma_{i}
to find the lowest
\delta_{i}
such that
G[\mathcal{S}q\leq\mathcal{T}_{\delta_{i}}\mathcal{S}q](Im(f-g))=\{0\}
.

Proof.

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[p\leq r]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[r\leq q]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G[\mathcal{S}p\leq\mathcal{S}r]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G[\mathcal{S}r\leq\mathcal{S}q]}

By Thm.
3.4
we know that
d^{G}_{\mathcal{S}q}
, then,

d_{\mathcal{S}q}^{G}(c,c^{\prime\prime})\leq\max\left\{d_{\mathcal{S}q}^{G}(c,c^{\prime}),d_{\mathcal{S}q}^{G}(c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime})\right\}.

One one hand the rightmost square is

d_{\mathcal{S}q}^{G}(c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime})\leq\max\left\{d_{\mathcal{S}q}^{G}(c^{\prime},c),d_{\mathcal{S}q}^{G}(c,c^{\prime\prime})\right\}

Finally for statement 3), assume that
\mathcal{T}
is an Archimedean flow. Since
c=G[\mathcal{S}r\leq\mathcal{S}q](b)
and
c^{\prime}=G[\mathcal{S}r\leq\mathcal{S}q](b^{\prime})
by Lemma
3.7
there is
\delta
such that
d_{\mathcal{S}r}^{G}(b,b^{\prime})<d_{\mathcal{S}q}^{G}(c,c^{\prime})+\delta
. Hence, because
d^{G}_{\mathcal{S}q}
is an ultrametric,

d_{\mathcal{S}r}^{G}(b,b^{\prime})\leq\max\left\{d_{\mathcal{S}q}^{G}(c,c^{\prime\prime}),d_{\mathcal{S}q}^{G}(c^{\prime\prime},c^{\prime})\right\}+\delta

{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.02 3.15 L -1.89 3.15 L 0 0 L 2.91 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}^{r,q}(\varphi)\right\}+\delta
. Hence the statement 3) follows.
∎

To obtain inequalities that involve the triangle diagram we need a specific properties of the flow

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
p<q
in
P
such that
L_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.7}{$\displaystyle\hbox to7.33pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.93002pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.42 4.41 L -2.64 4.41 L 0 0 L 4.07 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\hbox to3.87pt{\vbox to2.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.565pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

and
L_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.7}{$\displaystyle\hbox to7.33pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.59 6.3 L 3.78 6.3 L 0 0 L 5.81 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptstyle\hbox to5.25pt{\vbox to3.59pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.79 3.15 L 1.89 3.15 L 0 0 L 2.91 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}^{p,q}(\psi)=0
.

1.

If
F[p\leq q]
is isomorphism, we have that

L_{\bigtriangledown}^{q}(\varphi,\psi)\leq L_{\bigtriangledown}^{p}(\varphi,\psi).

2.

If
\mathcal{T}
is an Archimedean flow (Defn.
2.8
), there is
\delta>0
such that

L_{\bigtriangledown}^{p}(\varphi,\psi)<L_{\bigtriangledown}^{q}(\varphi,\psi)+\delta.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, assume that
q\leq\mathcal{S}\mathcal{S}p
.
For the sake of brevity let
p^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}\mathcal{S}p
and
q^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}\mathcal{S}q
and consider the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(q^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\mathcal{S}p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\mathcal{S}q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[p\leq q]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[q\leq p^{\prime}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[p^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{q}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\\
b\\
b^{\prime}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\\
c\\
c^{\prime}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[p\leq q]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[q\leq p^{\prime}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[p^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{q}}

Given
a^{\prime}\in F(q)
, and because
F[p\leq q]
is an isomorphism, we can choose
a=F[p\leq q]^{-1}(a^{\prime})
.
Because the left triangle is
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown$}_{\varphi,\psi}(p)
, we have that
d_{p^{\prime}}^{F}(b^{\prime},b)\leq L_{\bigtriangledown}^{p}(\varphi,\psi)
.
In addition,
d_{q^{\prime}}^{F}(c,c^{\prime})\leq d_{p^{\prime}}^{F}(b^{\prime},b)
by Lemma
3.6
, so

d_{q^{\prime}}^{F}(c,c^{\prime})\leq L_{\bigtriangledown}^{p}(\varphi,\psi)
.
By definition of supremum it follows that
L_{\bigtriangledown}^{q}(\varphi,\psi)\leq L_{\bigtriangledown}^{p}(\varphi,\psi)
finishing statement (1).

For statement (2), assume that
\mathcal{T}
is an Archimedean flow and choose any
a\in F(p)
. By Lemma
3.7
given that
c=F[p^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}](b)
and
c^{\prime}=F[p^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}](b^{\prime})
there is
\delta
such that
d_{p^{\prime}}^{F}(b,b^{\prime})<d_{q^{\prime}}^{F}(c,c^{\prime})+\delta
, which then implies that
L_{\bigtriangledown}^{p}(\varphi,\psi)<L_{\bigtriangledown}^{q}(\varphi,\psi)+\delta
.
∎

.

is discrete,

Proof.

Fix

and

.
By Lem.
4.5
, we have

L_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.7}{$\displaystyle\hbox to7.33pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.93002pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
